Hyperbolic sine-Gordon model beyond the first threshold
Younes Zine

Abstract
. We study the hyperbolic sine-Gordon model, with a parameter β 2 > 0 \beta^{2}>0 , and its associated Gibbs dynamics on the two-dimensional torus. By introducing a physical space approach to the Fourier restriction norm method and establishing nonlinear dispersive smoothing for the imaginary multiplicative Gaussian chaos, we construct invariant Gibbs dynamics for the hyperbolic sine-Gordon model beyond the first threshold β 2 = 2 ​ π \beta^{2}=2\pi . The deterministic step of our argument hinges on establishing key bilinear estimates, featuring weighted bounds for cone multipliers. Moreover, the probabilistic component involves a careful analysis of the imaginary Gaussian multiplicative chaos and reduces to integrating singularities along space-time light cones. As a by-product of our proof, we identify β 2 = 6 ​ π \beta^{2}=6\pi as a critical threshold for the hyperbolic sine-Gordon model, which is quite surprising given that the associated parabolic model has a critical threshold at β 2 = 8 ​ π \beta^{2}=8\pi .



\nabla

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35L71, 60H15

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Hyperbolic sine-Gordon model

We consider
the following stochastic damped sine-Gordon equation (SdSG) on

\mathbb{T}^{2}=(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}
:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u+\gamma\sin(\beta u)=\sqrt{2}\xi\\
(u,\partial_{t}u)|_{t=0}=(u_{0},v_{0}),\end{cases}\qquad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2},

where
u
is a real-valued unknown,
\gamma
and
\beta
are non-zero real numbers and
\xi
denotes space-time white noise on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
with the space-time covariance formally given by

\mathbb{E}[\xi(x_{1},t_{1})\xi(x_{2},t_{2})]=\delta(x_{1}-x_{2})\delta(t_{1}-t_{2}).

The Gibbs measure associated with (
1.1
) formally reads

(1.2)

\displaystyle``d\vec{\rho}(u,v)=Z^{-1}e^{-E(u,v)}dudv".

Here,
Z=Z(\beta)

denotes a normalization constant
and

(1.3)

\displaystyle E(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\big(u(x)^{2}+|\nabla u(x)|^{2}+v(x)^{2}\big)dx-\frac{\gamma}{\beta}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\cos\big(\beta u(x)\big)dx

denotes the energy (= Hamiltonian) of the (deterministic undamped) sine-Gordon equation:

(1.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+(1-\Delta)u+\gamma\sin(\beta u)=0.

The Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}
in (
1.2
)
arises in various physical contexts such as two-dimensional Yukawa and Coulomb gases in statistical mechanics and the quantum sine-Gordon model in Euclidean quantum field theory.
We refer the readers to
[
3
,
19
,
52
,
38
,
59
,
65
,
66
,
83
,
60
]
and the references therein for more physical motivations and interpretations of the measure
\vec{\rho}
.
The dynamical model (
1.1
) then corresponds to the
so-called “canonical” stochastic quantization
[
84
]
of the quantum sine-Gordon model represented by the measure
\vec{\rho}
in (
1.2
). See the works
[
2
,
5
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
37
,
38
,
47
,
48
,
76
]
for constructions of the sine-Gordon model for various ranges of the parameter
\beta^{2}
.

In
[
76
]
, Oh, Robert, Sosoe, and Wang constructed the dynamics (
1.1
) in the range
0<\beta^{2}<2\pi
.
1
1

1

See also
[
75
]
for a pathwise well-posedness result on the stochastic hyperbolic undamped sine-Gordon equation with deterministic initial data.
We review this argument in Subsection
1.4
below. In the present work, our main goal is to further extend the well-posedness theory for (
1.1
) beyond the threshold
\beta^{2}=2\pi
.

1.2.
Setup and main result

Here, we state our main result regarding the construction of the dynamics (
1.1
)
associated with the Gibbsian initial data
\vec{\rho}
(
1.2
) for
\beta^{2}\geq 2\pi
.
To this end, we first fix some notations.
Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
let
\mu_{s}
denote
a Gaussian measure, formally defined by

(1.5)

\displaystyle d\mu_{s}=Z_{s}^{-1}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\|u\|_{{H}^{s}}^{2}}du

\displaystyle=Z_{s}^{-1}\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle n\rangle^{2s}|\widehat{u}_{n}|^{2}}d\widehat{u}_{n},

where
Z_{s}
is a normalization constant,

\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle=\big(1+|\,\cdot\,|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and
\widehat{u}_{n}
denotes the Fourier coefficient of
u

at the frequency
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
We define

(1.6)

\displaystyle\vec{\mu}_{s}=\mu_{s}\otimes\mu_{s-1}.

In particular, when
s=1
,
the measure
\vec{\mu}_{1}
is defined as
the induced probability measure
under the map:

\omega\in\Omega\longmapsto(u_{0}^{\omega},v_{0}^{\omega}),

where
u_{0}^{\omega}
and
v_{0}^{\omega}
are given by

(1.7)

u_{0}^{\omega}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{g_{n}(\omega)}{\langle n\rangle}e_{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{0}^{\omega}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}h_{n}(\omega)e_{n}.

Here,

e_{n}=(2\pi)^{-1}e^{in\cdot x}

and
\{g_{n},h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
denotes a family of independent standard
complex-valued Gaussian random variables such that
\overline{g_{n}}=g_{-n}
and
\overline{h_{n}}=h_{-n}
,

n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
It is easy to see that
\vec{\mu}_{1}=\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{0}
is supported on

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}):=H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

for
s<0
but not for
s\geq 0
.

With
(
1.3
),
(
1.5
), and
(
1.6
),
we can formally write
\vec{\rho}
in (
1.2
) as

(1.8)

\displaystyle d\vec{\rho}(u,v)\sim e^{\frac{\gamma}{\beta}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\cos(\beta u)dx}d\vec{\mu}_{1}(u,v).

In view of the roughness of the support of
\vec{\mu}_{1}
,
the nonlinear term in (
1.8
) is not well-defined
and thus a proper renormalization is required to give a meaning to (
1.8
).

Let
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}
be a smooth frequency projector
onto the frequencies
\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:|n|\leq N\}

given by the following Fourier multiplier:

(1.9)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}f}(n)=\chi_{N}(n)\widehat{f}(n).

Here,
\widehat{f}
denotes the spatial Fourier transform of
f
and
\chi_{N}(n)=\chi(N^{-1}n)
for
some fixed non-negative radial function

\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

such that
\chi
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\supp\chi\subset\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|\xi|\leq 1\}
, and
\chi\equiv 1

on
\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|\xi|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\}
.
Given
u_{0}=u_{0}^{\omega}
as in (
1.7
),
i.e.
\operatorname{law}(u_{0})=\mu_{1}
,
set
\sigma_{N}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, by setting

(1.10)

\displaystyle\sigma_{N}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}u_{0}(x)\big)^{2}\Big]=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{\chi_{N}(n)^{2}}{\langle n\rangle^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log N+o(1),

as
N\to\infty
,
uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
define
the truncated renormalized density:

(1.11)

\displaystyle R_{N}(u)=\frac{\gamma_{N}}{\beta}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\cos\big(\beta\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}u(x)\big)dx,

where
\gamma_{N}=\gamma_{N}(\beta)
is given by

(1.12)

\gamma_{N}(\beta)=e^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sigma_{N}}.

In particular, we have
\gamma_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
.
We then define the truncated renormalized Gibbs measure:

(1.13)

\displaystyle d\vec{\rho}_{N}(u,v)=Z_{N}^{-1}e^{R_{N}(u)}d\vec{\mu}_{1}(u,v)

for some normalization constant
Z_{N}=Z_{N}(\beta)\in(0,\infty)
. One then proves the existence of a measure
\vec{\rho}
such that

(1.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\vec{\rho}_{N}=\vec{\rho},

in the sense of total variation. See Lemma
5.1
in Section
5
below.

We now consider the following renormalized truncated SdSG dynamics:

(1.15)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u_{N}+\partial_{t}u_{N}+(1-\Delta)u_{N}+\gamma_{N}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}\big\{\sin(\beta\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}u_{N})\big\}=\sqrt{2}\xi,

with the truncated Gibbs measure initial data
\vec{\rho}_{N}
(
1.13
). Here,
\gamma_{N}
is as in (
1.12
). Our main result below proves, for some range of parameters
\beta^{2}
, the convergence of the sequence
(u_{N},\partial_{t}u_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
to a non-trivial stochastic process
(u,\partial_{t}u)
whose law is given by
\vec{\rho}
(
1.14
) at every time marginal. This process
u
is hence formally interpreted as the solution to the following renormalized SdSG equation

(1.16)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u+\infty\cdot\sin(\beta u)=\sqrt{2}\xi,

at statistical equilibrium.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
0<\beta^{2}<2\pi\Big(1+\frac{3\sqrt{241}-41}{122}\Big)\approx 2.046\pi
. Then,
the stochastic damped sine-Gordon equation (
1.16
) is almost surely globally well-posed with respect to the renormalized Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}
defined in (
1.14
) and the renormalized Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}
is invariant under the dynamics. More precisely, there exists a process
(u,\partial_{t}u)\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathcal{H}^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
2
2

2

Here,
C(\mathbb{R}_{+},X)
for a Banach space
X
is the space of continuous functions from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
X
, endowed with the compact-open topology.
for any small
\varepsilon>0
such that the solution
(u_{N},\partial_{t}u_{N})
to (
1.15
) converges to
(u,\partial_{t}u)
in
C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\mathcal{H}^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))

\vec{\rho}
-almost surely as
N\to\infty
. Moreover, for each
t\geq 0
, the law of
(u(t),\partial_{t}u(t))
is given by
\vec{\rho}
.

Theorem
1.1
is proved in Section
6
. It constitutes a first step towards building a physical space approach to study random wave equations. See Remarks
1.10
and
1.12
below.

1.3.
Prior works

In this subsection, we give a brief overview of the literature relevant to our problem.

1.3.1.
Random wave equations with polynomial nonlinearities

For power type nonlinearities, there has been spectacular progress in the study of the well-posedness issue for random wave equations in the recent years. In
[
45
]
, Gubinelli, Koch and Oh studied the following quadratic wave equation in three dimensions:

(1.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+(1-\Delta)u+u^{2}=\xi,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{3},

where
\xi
is space-time white noise on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
. By adapting the paracontrolled approach of Gubinelli, Imkeller and Perkowski
[
43
]
, developed for parabolic equations, to the wave setting together with the random operator perspective of Bourgain
[
10
]
, they proved well-posedness (for smooth enough initial data) of (
1.17
). A key ingredient in their argument is to prove the so-called
multilinear smoothing
for (a renormalized version of) the square of the stochastic convolution
\Psi
solving

\partial_{t}^{2}\Psi-\Delta\Psi=\xi.

More precisely, they prove that (a renormalized version of)
3
3

3

Here and in the rest of this subsection, we omit renormalization issues for the sake of simplicity.

\Psi^{2}
belongs to
C(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
for any
\varepsilon>0
, beating the initial guess
\Psi^{2}\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-1-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3}))
obtained by naive “parabolic power counting”; see
[
50
,
70
]
. This was achieved by using a simple, but crucial observation:

(1.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\Psi^{2})=\mathcal{F}(\Psi)*\mathcal{F}(\Psi),

where
\mathcal{F}
denotes the spatial Fourier transform; reducing the argument to a Fourier-space analysis.

In
[
12
,
13
]
, Bringmann further developed the Fourier-based methodology of
[
45
]
and considered the following Hartree cubic nonlinear wave equation in three dimensions:

(1.19)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}u+(1-\Delta)u-(\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\alpha}*u^{2})u=0\\
(u,\partial_{t}u)|_{t=0}=(u_{0},v_{0}),\end{cases}\qquad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{3},

for
\alpha>0
and where the rough random initial data
(u_{0},v_{0})
is sampled from the Hartree
\Phi^{4}_{3}
Gibbs measure. By adapting the Fourier norm restriction norm method of Bourgain and Kleinerman-Machedon
[
6
,
7
,
56
]
to the random wave context and reducing the multilinear smoothing discussed above to counting estimates, he proved almost sure global well-posedness for (
1.19
) and invariance of the Hartree
\Phi^{4}_{3}
Gibbs measure under the dynamics for any
\alpha>0
.

The developments in the polynomial setting eventually culminated in the breakthrough work
[
15
]
, where Bringmann, Deng, Nahmod and Yue proved almost sure global well-posedness for the hyperbolic
\Phi^{4}_{3}
-model (namely, (
1.19
) with
\alpha=0
) and invariance of the corresponding
\Phi^{4}_{3}
-measure under the dynamics, by mixing the paracontrolled approach together with inputs from the theory of random tensors
[
27
]
and the molecule analysis of
[
25
]
.

See also
[
11
,
16
,
17
,
29
,
44
,
46
,
63
,
77
,
80
,
72
,
73
,
74
,
81
,
96
,
99
]
and references therein for other works on the well-posedness issue for other random wave models.

1.3.2.
Parabolic sine-Gordon model.

In
[
19
]
, Chandra, Hairer and Shen considered the parabolic counterpart to (
1.1
):

(1.20)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u+\gamma\sin(\beta u)=\sqrt{2}\xi\\
u|_{t=0}=u_{0},\end{cases}\qquad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}.

They proved local well-posedness for (
1.20
) in the full subcritical range
0<\beta^{2}<8\pi
in
[
19
,
52
]
by adapting the theory of regularity structures
[
50
]
to the sine nonlinearity setting (see also
[
52
]
for a partial result). In
[
14
]
, Bringmann and Cao globalized the solutions constructed in
[
19
]
in the restricted range
0<\beta^{2}<6\pi
. See also
[
18
]
.

We refer the reader to Remark
1.3
below for a discussion on differences between the wave and heat sine-Gordon models.

Remark 1.2
.

It is tempting to adapt the Fourier-based methods of the works
[
13
,
15
,
45
]
on random wave equations with polynomial nonlinearities discussed in Subsection
1.3
to the sine nonlinearity setting of (
1.36
). However, formulas of the form (
1.18
), which are a cornerstone of the aforementioned approaches, do not hold in the non-polynomial setup. Namely, we cannot directly rely the Fourier transform of
\Theta_{N}
to that of
\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}
. Furthermore, taking inspiration from the literature
[
19
,
52
]
on the parabolic counterpart (
1.20
) to (
1.1
), it is natural to develop a physical-side framework to study the wave sine-Gordon dynamics in order to take advantage of the key properties of the sine nonlinearity (boundedness and Lipschitz continuity).

Remark 1.3
.

Let us highlight key differences between the hyperbolic and parabolic sine-Gordon model. First of all, parabolic flows enjoys a much stronger smoothing property than wave flows. Furthermore, while on the one hand, heat equations are compatible with
L^{\infty}
type spaces, wave equations on the other hand, are only compatible with a
L^{2}
analysis. This leads to integrability issues; see for instance Remark
1.5
. From a more technical perspective, implementing a physical space approach for the hyperbolic sine-Gordon requires to handle singularities along light cones as opposed to singularities at single points in the parabolic case; see the discussion in the next subsection. These reasons explain why the analysis of the hyperbolic model is much harder than its parabolic counterpart.

1.4.
Main challenges and ideas

Here, we discuss the proof of Theorem
1.1
. In view of the absolute continuity of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}
with respect to the Gaussian measure
\vec{\mu}_{1}
, we consider (
1.16
) with the Gaussian random data
(u_{0},v_{0})
and
\Law(u_{0},v_{0})=\vec{\mu}_{1}
as in (
1.7
). In particular, for
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the solution
u_{N}
to (
1.15
) with initial data given by
(u_{0},v_{0})
.

1.4.1.
First order expansion

We first proceed with the following first order expansion (
[
10
,
23
,
76
]
):

(1.21)

\displaystyle u_{N}=\Psi^{\textup{KG}}+v_{N},

where
\Psi^{\text{KG}}
is the solution to the following linear damped wave equation:

(1.22)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}\Psi^{\textup{KG}}+\partial_{t}\Psi^{\textup{KG}}+(1-\Delta)\Psi^{\textup{KG}}=\sqrt{2}\xi\\
(\Psi^{\textup{KG}},\partial_{t}\Psi^{\textup{KG}})|_{t=0}=(u_{0},v_{0}),\end{cases}

where
\Law(u_{0},v_{0})=\vec{\mu}_{1}
.
Define the linear damped Klein-Gordon propagator
\mathcal{D}(t)
by

(1.23)

\mathcal{D}(t)=e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\frac{\sin(t[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt])}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt]},

where

[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]=\Big(\frac{3}{4}+|n|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},

as a Fourier multiplier operator.
Then, we have

(1.24)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\textup{KG}}(t)=\partial_{t}\mathcal{D}(t)u_{0}+\mathcal{D}(t)(u_{0}+v_{0})+\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})d\mathcal{W}(t^{\prime}),

where
\mathcal{W}
denotes a cylindrical Wiener process on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
:

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(t)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}B_{n}(t)e_{n},

and

\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}

is defined by

B_{n}(0)=0
and

B_{n}(t)=\langle\xi,\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}\cdot e_{n}\rangle_{t,x}
.
Here,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{t,x}
denotes
the duality pairing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
As a result,
we see that
\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is a family of mutually independent complex-valued
4
4

4

In particular,
B_{0}
is a standard real-valued Brownian motion.

Brownian motions such that
B_{-n}=\overline{B_{n}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
By convention, we normalize
B_{n}
such that
\text{Var}(B_{n}(t))=t
.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N}
be the truncated stochastic convolution:

(1.26)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}\Psi^{\textup{KG}}.

A direct computation shows that
\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N}(t,x)

is a mean-zero real-valued Gaussian random variable with variance

(1.27)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N}(t,x)^{2}\big]=\sigma_{N}

for any
t\geq 0
,
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
where
\sigma_{N}
is as in (
1.10
). Moreover, one can show that
\{\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
C([0,T];W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
, almost surely for any
T,\varepsilon>0
; see Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
. Hence, it converges to
\Psi^{\textup{KG}}
in
C([0,T];W^{-\varepsilon,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
, almost surely.

For reasons discussed in Remark
1.8
below, we actually work with the following wave stochastic convolution:

(1.28)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\text{wave}}(t)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\mathcal{S}(t)u_{0}+\mathcal{S}(t)(u_{0}+v_{0})+\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{S}(t-t^{\prime})d\mathcal{W}(t^{\prime}),

(1.29)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}\Psi^{\textup{wave}},\quad N\in\mathbb{N}.

Here,
\mathcal{S}
is the linear propagator associated to the damped wave equation. Namely,
\mathcal{S}
is given by

(1.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(t)=e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\frac{\sin(t|\nabla|)}{|\nabla|}.

We also define for
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the truncated stochastic convolution
\Psi^{\text{wave}}_{N}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}\Psi^{\text{wave}}
and observe that

(1.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}(t,x)^{2}\big]=\sigma_{N}+O(1),

where
O(1)
is a constant which is uniform in
N
. We also show in Lemma
5.4
that the difference
\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N}-\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}
is a smooth enough function (uniformly in
N
) for our purposes.

The nonlinear remainder
v_{N}=u_{N}-\Psi^{\textup{KG}}
satistfies the following equation:

(1.32)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}v_{N}+\partial_{t}v_{N}+(1-\Delta)v_{N}=-\gamma_{N}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}\big\{\sin(\beta\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}v_{N}+\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N})\big\},

with the zero initial data. By expanding the nonlinearity using trigonometric formulas, the mild formulation for (
1.32
) reads

(1.33)

\displaystyle v_{N}=-\sum_{\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1}\in\{+,-\}}c_{\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1}}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}\mathcal{I}\Big(e^{i\varepsilon_{1}\beta\mathbf{\Pi}_{\leq N}v_{N}}e^{i\beta(\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N}-\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N})}\cdot\Theta^{\varepsilon_{0}}_{N}\Big),

where
c_{\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1}}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\Theta_{N}^{\varepsilon_{0}}
is the imaginary Gaussian multiplicative chaos

(1.34)

\displaystyle\Theta_{N}^{\varepsilon_{0}}=\gamma_{N}e^{i\varepsilon_{0}\beta\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}}=e^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sigma_{N}}e^{i\varepsilon_{0}\beta\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}}.

and
\mathcal{I}
is the Duhamel operator

(1.35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(F)(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})F(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime},\quad t\geq 0.

By proceeding as in

[
52
,
75
,
76
]
,
we establish the regularity property of
\Theta^{\varepsilon_{0}}_{N}
;
see Lemma
5.14
.
In particular, given
0<\beta^{2}<4\pi
,

\{\Theta^{\varepsilon_{0}}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

forms a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p}(\Omega;L^{q}([0,T];W^{-\alpha,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})))

for any finite
p,q\geq 1
and
\alpha>\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}
; see Lemma
5.14
.

1.4.2.
The first threshold
\beta^{2}=2\pi

We quickly describe the argument in
[
76
]
which leads to the restriction
\beta^{2}<2\pi
. In what follows, we work with the following simplified equation for
v_{N}
for convenience:

(1.36)

\displaystyle v_{N}=-\mathcal{I}\big(e^{iv_{N}+\Psi_{N}}\cdot\Theta_{N}\big),

with
\Psi_{N}=\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N}-\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}
and
\Theta_{N}=\Theta^{+}_{N}
or
\Theta^{-}_{N}
. Our goal is to solve (
1.36
) in
L_{t}^{\infty}H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for some
s>0
to be determined. To this end, we analyze the different frequency interactions on the right-hand-side of (
1.36
):

(1.37)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\big(\mathbf{P}_{N_{0}}\big(\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}\big(e^{iv_{N}+\Psi_{N}}\big)\cdot\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}\Theta_{N}\big)\big),

where
(N_{0},N_{1},N_{2})\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{3}
and
\mathbf{P}_{K}
denotes a smooth spatial projection onto frequencies
\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:|n|\sim K\}
; see (
2.38
) below. In view of the regularity
5
5

5

In the rest of this subsection, all stated regularities are understood to be on a set of full probability and uniform in
N
.

(1.38)

\displaystyle\Theta_{N}\in L^{\infty}_{t}W_{x}^{-\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}-\varepsilon,\infty}

for small
\varepsilon>0
,
the inhomogeneous estimate

(1.39)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}(F)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s+1}_{x}}\lesssim\|F\|_{L_{t}^{1}H^{s}_{x}}

and standard product estimates, analyzing the frequency localized product (
1.37
) leads to the following observations:

(LH)

low
\times
high
\to
high interaction:
N_{1}\ll N_{0}\sim N_{2}
.
We need
s-1+\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}+\varepsilon<0
to handle this case;

(HH)

high
\times
high interaction:
N_{1}\sim N_{2}
.
We need
s>\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}+\varepsilon
to handle this case.

Therefore, combining the cases (LH) and (HH) yields the condition
\beta^{2}<2\pi
for
\varepsilon
small enough.

1.4.3.
An interpolation argument

Without loss of generality, we fix
2\pi\leq\beta^{2}<4\pi
. Our main idea is to improve on the restriction
s-1+\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}+\varepsilon<0
via an interpolation argument which we outline next. We further divide the (LH) interaction case into two subcases. Fix
0<\gamma<1
(to be chosen small later) and consider the cases (LH1) and (LH2) as follows:

(LH1)

(not too low)
\times
high
\to
high interaction:
N_{0}^{\gamma}\leq N_{1}\ll N_{0}\sim N_{2}
.

(LH2)

(very low)
\times
high interaction:
N_{1}<N_{0}^{\gamma}
.

By taking advantage of the high space-time integrability of the chaos
\Theta_{N}
(see (
1.38
)), we may borrow derivatives from
e^{iv_{N}+\Psi_{N}}
in the (LH1) case. Consequently, this interaction can be placed in
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for

(1.40)

\displaystyle s-1-\gamma s+\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}+\varepsilon<0;

see Lemma
4.10
.

The crucial step in our argument is to improve on the restriction on
s
in the case (LH2): note that using the information (
1.38
) as in the case (LH) would yield the condition
s-1+\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}+\varepsilon<0
again (because of the scenario
N_{1}\sim 1
). We instead (essentially) use the new information

(1.41)

\displaystyle\Theta_{N}\in Y^{-\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}+\frac{1}{2}-3\varepsilon,-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}_{-\frac{1}{2}-3\varepsilon},

where
Y^{s,b}_{a}
for
(s,b,a)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the
L^{2}
-based space associated with a weighted variant of the usual Fourier restriction norm:

\|u\|_{Y_{a}^{s,b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}:=\big\|\langle t\rangle^{a}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{t,x}\big(\langle\zeta\rangle^{s}\,||\tau|-|\zeta||^{b}\widehat{u}(\tau,\zeta)\big)\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})},

where
\widehat{u}
and
\mathcal{F}_{t,x}^{-1}[u]
respectively denote the space-time Fourier transform and its inverse. See Subsection
2.3
.

Remark 1.4
.

The bound (
1.41
) represents a
\frac{1}{2}
-gain of spatial derivatives as compared to (
1.38
). This is similar in spirit to the multilinear smoothing phenomenon in the polynomial case discussed in Subsection
1.3
. We thus refer to it as nonlinear smoothing for the imaginary Gaussian multiplicative chaos
\Theta_{N}
.

We defer the discussion on the ideas behind the proof of (
1.41
) to the end of the subsection and explain how to use it to study (LH2). By using (
1.41
) together with the Fourier restriction norm (namely
X^{s,b}
-spaces; see Subsection
2.3
) and a duality argument, the contribution of the interaction (LH2) is bounded by an expression of the form

(1.42)

\displaystyle\big\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{0}}w\cdot\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}\big(e^{iv_{N}+\Psi_{N}}\big)\big\|_{Y^{-\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}+\frac{1}{2}-3\varepsilon,-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}_{-\frac{1}{2}-3\varepsilon}},

where
w
belongs to
X^{1-s,\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
. Morally speaking, the bound (
1.41
) essentially allowed us to trade a
\frac{1}{2}
-modulation derivatives for
\frac{1}{2}
-spatial derivatives in (
1.42
). The key upside of such a trade lies in the following observation: when estimating a product of two functions
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
, modulation derivatives
6
6

6

Namely, the mixed symbol
||\tau|-|\zeta||
, where
(\zeta,\tau)
is the space-time Fourier variable of the product
u_{1}u_{2}
.
cost in general a lot less than spatial derivatives when estimating products; see Lemma
4.9
. This essentially leads to the bound

\displaystyle\eqref{prodo}\lesssim N_{0}^{s-1+\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon+C\gamma}\cdot\|v_{N}\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}}\|w\|_{X^{1-s,\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}}

for some constant
C>0
, which in turn gives the restriction

(1.43)

\displaystyle s-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}+\varepsilon+C\gamma<0.

For
\gamma
small enough and
\beta^{2}<3\pi
, the condition (
1.40
) is more restrictive than (
1.43
). Therefore, the case (LH) can be handled under (
1.40
), which together with (HH) yields

\beta^{2}<\frac{4\pi}{2-\gamma},

which is an improvement over the restriction
\beta^{2}<2\pi
.

In practice, we optimize the value of
\gamma
which leads to the specific improved range of parameters
\beta^{2}
in Theorem
1.1
. The rigorous interpolation argument is implemented by proving bilinear estimates which follow from a careful multi-parameter analysis; see Section
4
.

Remark 1.5

(On the constant
C
in (
1.43
))
.

In the strategy described above, an improvement on the constant
C
(making it as small as possible) directly leads to an improvement on the range of parameters
\beta^{2}
. In our approach, the main issue in minimizing
C
comes from the fact that we are trying to estimate a product of two
L^{2}
functions in a
L^{2}
space. Therefore, by Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev’s embedding we necessarily lose a full power of
N_{1}
, which implies
C\geq 1
. In fact, within our framework,
C
has to be much larger than one, as we need to place
e^{iv_{N}+\Psi_{N}}
in a
L^{p}
-based anisotropic Sobolev space, with
1<p<2
, which enjoys a fractional chain rule, to prove relevant difference estimates in our well-posedness argument; see Proposition
6.1
in Section
6
.

Remark 1.6

(Analysis in weighted
Y^{s,b}_{a}
spaces)
.

The presence of (time) weights in
Y^{s,b}_{a}
-norms renders our nonlinear analysis particularly challenging. We employ techniques from harmonic analysis to overcome this issue and, in particular, prove a weighted
L^{2}
estimate for a cone multiplier; see Subsection
4.2
.

1.4.4.
Nonlinear smoothing for the imaginary Gaussian multiplicative chaos

We now discuss the proof of the nonlinear smoothing bound (
1.41
); which is the main probabilistic step of our work and requires a careful analysis on the physical side. The main step reduces to showing the following second moment estimate:

(1.44)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\vec{\mu}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{P}}\Big[\big|\big(\Box^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}\,|\nabla_{x}|(\mathbf{P}_{N_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{[0,1]}\Theta_{N})\big)(t,x)\big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}N_{0}^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2\pi}+\varepsilon}\langle t\rangle^{4\varepsilon}

for any
N_{0}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Here,
\Box^{-b}
is the so-called hyperbolic Riesz potential; see (
2.27
) and (
2.34
). The main advantage of the expression (
1.44
) lies in the fact that all the convolution kernels of the multipliers on its left-hand-side have an explicit physical side representation. This is crucial to exploit the cancellation properties of the chaos
\Theta_{N}
.

Remark 1.7
.

In reducing (
1.41
) to (
1.44
), we need to take the
L^{2}_{t}
-norm of the square root of the right-hand-side of (
1.44
). The presence of the weight in the
Y^{s,b}_{a}
-norm in (
1.41
) ensures that this
L^{2}_{t}
-norm converges.

The bound (
1.44
) follows from three new ingredients:

(i)

Sharp estimates on the space-time covariance of
\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}
.

(ii)

A multi-variate Sobolev argument.

(iii)

Integrating singularities along light cones.

We briefly discuss (i), (ii) and (iii). Let us start with (i). Since the chaos
\Theta_{N}
involves the stochastic convolution
\Psi_{N}^{\textup{wave}}
, proving (
1.44
) requires a fine understanding of the space-time covariance of
\Psi_{N}^{\textup{wave}}
given by

(1.45)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{N}(t_{1},t_{2},x_{1},x_{2})=\mathbb{E}\big[\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}(t_{1},x_{1})\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}(t_{2},x_{2})\big]

for any
(t_{1},x_{1}),(t_{2},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
. In Proposition
5.5
, we prove the following two-sided bound on
\Gamma_{N}
:

(1.46)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{N}(t_{1},t_{2},x_{1},x_{2})=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\big(|t_{1}-t_{2}|+|x_{1}-x_{2}|+N^{-1}\big)+R_{N}(t_{1},t_{2},x_{1},x_{2}),

where
R_{N}
is bounded uniformly in
N
. Note that the singularity on the right-hand-side of (
1.46
) is of
elliptic
type, in the sense that it is singular at the space-time origin (in the limit
N\to\infty
). In view of the derivative term on the left-hand-side of (
1.44
), we also need to estimate the spatial derivatives of
\Gamma_{N}
. When differentiating the remainder term
R_{N}
,
hyperbolic singularities
along light cones of the form

(1.47)

\displaystyle(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\mapsto||t|-|x||^{-c},\quad c\in\mathbb{N},

show up. This is in sharp contrast with the parabolic case
[
19
,
52
]
where the remainder term is smooth; see Remarks
5.6
and
5.8
for a more thorough discussion on this point. Proving the required bounds (
1.46
) and particularly its variant with derivatives (see Proposition
5.7
) is very challenging as one needs to keep track of subtle cancellations within the covariance function
\Gamma_{N}
and handle the hyperbolic singularities (
1.47
) effectively; see Section
3
and Subsection
5.2
.

Remark 1.8
.

We analyze the covariance of the stochastic convolution
\Psi^{\textup{wave}}_{N}
(as opposed to
\Psi^{\textup{KG}}_{N}
) as it is constructed from a kernel with an explicit formula on the physical side; see (
2.24
).

The ingredient (ii) comes from the following observation: in bounding the left-hand-side of (
1.44
), we need to estimate a quantity of the form

(1.48)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x_{1}}||\nabla_{x_{2}}|\mathbf{P}^{x_{1}}_{N_{0}}\mathbf{P}^{x_{2}}_{N_{0}}\operatorname{Cov}(\Theta_{N},\Theta_{N})(t_{1},x_{1},t_{2},x_{2}),

where
|\nabla_{x_{\ell}}|
and
\mathbf{P}_{N_{0}}^{x_{\ell}}
are the multipliers
|\nabla|
and
\mathbf{P}_{N_{0}}
along the variable
x_{\ell}
for
\ell=1,2
and
\operatorname{Cov}(\Theta_{N},\Theta_{N})
is the space-time covariance of
\Theta_{N}
and is given by
e^{\beta^{2}\Gamma_{N}}
. In estimating (
1.48
), one may move both derivatives either (a) to the (kernels of)
\mathbf{P}^{x_{\ell}}_{N_{0}}
for
\ell=1,2
or (b) to the covariance function
\operatorname{Cov}(\Theta_{N},\Theta_{N})
. Scenario (a) gives a factor
N_{0}^{2}
, which is not allowed in (
1.44
) (since
\beta^{2}<4\pi
) and Scenario (b) is also problematic since it outputs second order derivatives of the covariance
\Gamma_{N}
, which are not locally integrable functions; see Proposition
5.7
.

Our argument (ii) overcomes this issue by interpolating between the two cases (a) and (b). It yields the correct power of
N_{0}
allowed on the right-hand-side of (
1.44
) and a locally integrable function made of a singularity of the form (
1.47
) mixed with an elliptic singularity; see Subsection
5.5
for more details.

Lastly, the third ingredient (iii) allows us to integrate the product of the singularity output in the Sobolev argument (ii) and the kernel of the operator
\Box^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
which is also a mix of a hyperbolic and an elliptic singularity; see (
2.33
). Bounding the resulting integrals boils down to carefully estimating the volume of the intersection of transverse tubes in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
; see Subsection
5.6
. This is the physical side counterpart of the counting arguments in the Fourier-based literature on random wave equations; see for instance
[
13
,
15
,
45
]
.

A computation (see Remark
5.20
) with (
1.46
) shows that
\Theta_{N}
does not converge as a space-time distribution in the limit
N\to\infty
for
\beta^{2}\geq 6\pi
. This is an instance of the so-called “variance blowup”; see
[
30
,
71
,
51
]
. This suggests the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.9
.

The renormalized sine-Gordon model (
1.16
) is globally well-posed on the support of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}
for
\beta^{2}<6\pi
.

1.5.
Final remarks

We conclude this section with a few remarks.

Remark 1.10

(Further progress on Conjecture
1.9
)
.

In the forthcoming work
[
101
]
, we make a further progress to
1.9
by going beyond the
L^{2}
analysis of the present paper. Our method uses insights from recent developments in the Fourier restriction theory for the cone
[
49
]
.

Remark 1.11

(Thresholds for the hyperbolic sine-Gordon model)
.

We emphasize that in this work, the “first threshold” for the hyperbolic sine-Gordon equation refers to the
analytical
threshold
\beta^{2}=2\pi
, where the basic first-order expansion argument in
[
76
]
provides a starting point for the well-posedness theory of (
1.16
). This is different from the first
physical
threshold
\beta^{2}=4\pi
at which another further renormalization of the stochastic objects is needed to define the dynamics; see
[
52
]
. It is not clear at this point what is the range of
\beta^{2}
for which one would need to go beyond a first order expansion to solve (
1.16
), although our analysis seems to suggest that
\beta^{2}=3\pi
is a natural candidate in view of (
1.43
) for example.

Remark 1.12

(Physical space methods for other models)
.

It would be of interest to apply the physical space methods developed in this paper to other hyperbolic models. Besides other equations with non-polynomial nonlinearities such as the Liouville model considered in
[
78
]
, wave equations in non-homogeneous settings are natural candidates for this endeavor. For instance, it would be interest to study (
1.17
) aas in
[
45
]
, but with
\mathbb{T}^{3}
replaced with a general three dimensional manifold; see the work
[
77
]
for an example of the analysis of a singular wave equations with a general input manifold.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce our set of notations, function spaces and state basic estimates. In Section
3
, we prove estimates on elliptic and hyperbolic kernels that are needed later in the paper. Next, in Sections
4
and
5
, we respectively prove key bilinear estimates and construct the stochastic objects that are used in our fixed point argument. In Section
6
, we state a global well-posedness statement and prove Theorem
1.1
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notations

In this subsection,
we introduce some notations.
We then set our conventions for the Fourier transforms
and state some basic facts.

\bullet
Preliminary notations.
We write
A\lesssim B
to denote an estimate of the form
A\leq CB
.
Similarly, we write
A\sim B
to denote
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
and use
A\ll B

when we have
A\leq cB
for small
c>0
. We may write
A\lesssim_{\theta}B
for
A\leq CB
with
C=C(\theta)

if we want to emphasize the dependence of the implicit constant on some parameter
\theta
.
We use
C,c>0
, etc. to denote various constants whose values may change line by line.

Given two functions
f
and
g
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M}
, with
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
or
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, we write

(2.1)

\displaystyle f\approx g,

if there exist
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f(t,x)+c_{1}\leq g(x)\leq f(t,x)+c_{2}
for any
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M}\setminus\{0\}
.
Similarly, given two sequences
\{f_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{g_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

of functions, we write

(2.2)

\displaystyle f_{N}\approx g_{N},

if there exist
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, independent of
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
such that
f_{N}(t,x)+c_{1}\leq g_{N}(t,x)\leq f_{N}(t,x)+c_{2}
for any
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{M}\setminus\{0\}
.

Given a set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathbf{1}_{A}
the indicator function of
A
, by
\#A
its cardinality and by
|A|
its Lebesgue measure. Given a metric space
X
, we use
B(x_{0},r)\subset X
to denote the open ball of radius

r>0
centered at
x_{0}\in X
.

For
x,y\in\mathbb{T}^{2}\cong[-\pi,\pi)^{2}
,
we set

|x-y|_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}=\min_{k\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}^{2}}|x-y+k|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}},

where
|\cdot|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
denotes the standard Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
When there is no confusion,
we simply use
|\cdot|
for both
|\cdot|_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}
and
|\cdot|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
.

We set
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and use the shorthand notation
\mathbb{Z}^{d}_{\geq 0}
for
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{d}
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Capital letters will sometimes denote dyadic numbers; namely, we write

N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
and
L\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
, for example.

Given
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
we set
a\vee b=\max(a,b)

and
a\wedge b=\min(a,b)
.

Let
t>0
. We denote by
\mathbb{S}^{1}(t)
the circle of centre
0
and radius
t
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(or in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, depending on the context). We will also use the notation
\mathbb{S}^{1}
for
\mathbb{S}^{1}(1)
.

Throughout the paper, we use the standard multi-index notation. Namely, we call multi-index a vector of the form
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
and write
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
for its
\ell^{1}
norm. We also use the standard notation
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}
for the derivative
\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{x_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}
in the canonical coordinate system
x=(x_{1},x_{2})
in
\mathcal{M}
, with
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}^{2}
or
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

\bullet
Fourier transforms.

Due to the nature of our analysis, constants play important roles.
In particular, the sharp bounds on the space-time covariance of
the truncated stochastic convolution (
1.29
)
(see Proposition
5.5
below)
require us to carefully fix our conventions for Fourier transforms.

We denote by
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{-1}

the spatial Fourier transform on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and its inverse, respectively,
which are given by

(2.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(f)(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x)e^{-i\xi\cdot x}dx,\qquad{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}^{-1}(f)(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\xi)e^{i\xi\cdot x}d\xi.

We then define the convolution product on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

(2.4)

\displaystyle(f\ast g)(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(y)g(x-y)dy

such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(f\ast g)={\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(f){\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g)
. Similarly,
the Fourier transform
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}
on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
is given by

(2.5)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(f)(n)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f(x)\overline{e_{n}(x)}dx,\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},

where

(2.6)

\displaystyle e_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{in\cdot x}.

Then, the Fourier inversion formula reads as

(2.7)

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(f)(n)e_{n}(x),

We define
the convolution product on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
by

(2.8)

\displaystyle(f\ast g)(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f(y)g(x-y)dy,

such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(f\ast g)={\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(f){\mathcal{F}}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(g)
.
We also define the space-time Fourier transform on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

by setting

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{t,x}(u)(\tau,\zeta)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(t,x)e^{-i(t\tau+\zeta\cdot x)}dtdx.

Then,
the inverse Fourier transform is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{t,x}(u)(t,x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(\tau,\zeta)e^{i(t\tau+\zeta\cdot x)}d\tau d\zeta.

In the following,
when it is clear from the context,
we write
{\mathcal{F}}(f)
and
\widehat{f}
for the Fourier transform of a function
f
defined either on

\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, and
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
A similar comment applies to
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(f)
and
\widecheck{f}
.

Next, we recall the Poisson summation formula; see
[
41
, Theorem 3.2.8]
.
Let
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that (i)
there exists
\eta>0
such that

|f(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-2-\eta}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and (ii)
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}|\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(f)(n)|<\infty
. Then, we have

(2.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(f)(n)e_{n}(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}f(x+2\pi k)

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Let
d\sigma
denote the normalized surface measure on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
and

\widecheck{d\sigma}
denotes its inverse Fourier transform defined by

(2.10)

\displaystyle\widecheck{d\sigma}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}e^{i\omega\cdot x}d\sigma(\omega).

Then, it follows from
[
87
, Theorem 1.2.1]
that

(2.11)

\displaystyle\widecheck{d\sigma}(x)=e^{i|x|}a_{+}(x)+e^{-i|x|}a_{-}(x),

where the functions
a_{\pm}
are smooth and

(2.12)

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a_{\pm}(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}-|\alpha|}

for any multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
.
See also
[
41
, Appendix B.8]
.

\bullet
Sobolev spaces.
Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the
L^{2}
-based Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is defined by the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{s}}=\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}=\|\langle n\rangle^{s}\widehat{f}(n)\|_{\ell^{2}_{n}}.

We also use the notation
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
the
L^{p}
-based Sobolev
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is defined by the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{s,p}}=\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{p}_{x}}=\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big[\langle n\rangle^{s}\widehat{f}(n)\big] \!\big\|_{L^{p}_{x}}.

We define similarly the (time) Sobolev spaces
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R})
.

2.2.
Multiplier operators
and frequency projectors

In this subsection, we

the hyperbolic Riesz potentials

\bullet
Green’s functions and Bessel potentials.

The Green’s function
G_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
for
1-\Delta
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, satisfying

(1-\Delta)G_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=\delta_{0}^{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
, where
\delta_{0}^{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
is the Dirac delta function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
is given by

(2.13)

\displaystyle\widehat{G}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi\langle\xi\rangle^{2}},\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

on the Fourier side.
Recall from
[
42
, Proposition 1.2.5]

that
G_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

is a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
and decays exponentially as
|x|\to\infty
.
Furthermore, it satisfies

(2.14)

\displaystyle G_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(x)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|+o(1),

as
x\to 0
; see
[
1
, (4,2)]
and we have the estimate

(2.15)

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}G(x)|\lesssim_{\alpha}\begin{cases}\langle\,\log(|x|)\rangle\mathbf{1}_{|\alpha|=0}+|x|^{-|\alpha|}\mathbf{1}_{|\alpha|>0}&\quad\text{if $x\in B(0,2)\setminus\{0\}$},\\
e^{-c|x|}&\quad\text{if $|x|\geq 2$},\end{cases}

for some constant
c>0
and any multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
.

Now, let
G
be the Green’s
function for
1-\Delta
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
In view of our normalization (
2.6
), we have

(2.16)

\displaystyle\widehat{G}(n)=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\big(1-\Delta)^{-1}\delta_{0}\big)(n)=\frac{1}{2\pi\langle n\rangle^{2}},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},

where
\delta_{0}
denotes the Dirac delta function on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.
Moreover, by applying the Poisson summation formula (
2.9
), we obtain

(2.17)

\displaystyle G(x)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|+R(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus\{0\},

for a smooth function
R
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

Given
\alpha>0
, let
\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\alpha}

be the Bessel potential of order
\alpha
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
given by

(2.18)

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\alpha}f=J_{\alpha}*f,

where the convolution kernel
J_{\alpha}
is given by

(2.19)

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{\alpha}}e_{n}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}.

Then, given
0<\alpha<2
,
it follows from
[
75
, Lemma 2.2]
that
there exists
a smooth function
R_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
such that

(2.20)

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}(x)=c_{\alpha}|x|^{\alpha-2}+R_{\alpha}(x),

for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\cong[-\pi,\pi)^{2}\setminus\{0\}
.

In Subsection
5.4
, we need the Bessel potential in the temporal variable;
let
\langle\partial_{t}\rangle^{-\alpha}
,
\alpha>0
, be
the Fourier multiplier operator
with the multiplier
\langle\tau\rangle^{-\alpha}

whose convolution kernel
J_{\alpha}^{(t)}
is given by

(2.21)

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}^{(t)}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\langle\tau\rangle^{\alpha}}e^{it\tau}d\tau,\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

Recall from

[
42
, Proposition 1.2.5]

that

J_{\alpha}^{(t)}
is a smooth, strictly positive function on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.
Moreover,
for

0<\alpha<1
, there exists
c>0
such that

(2.22)

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}^{(t)}(t)\lesssim\begin{cases}e^{-|t|},&\text{for }|t|\geq 2,\\
|t|^{\alpha-1},&\text{for }|t|<2.\end{cases}

\bullet
Fractional derivation.
Consider the Fourier multiplier
(-\partial^{2}_{\tau})^{s}
, for
0<s<\frac{1}{2}
, on
\mathbb{R}
:

\mathcal{F}_{\tau}[(-\partial^{2}_{\tau})^{s}f](t)=|t|^{2s}\widehat{f}(t),\quad\text{for $t\in\mathbb{R}$}.

The operator
(-\partial^{2}_{\tau})^{s}
has the following integral representation; see
[
91
, Theorem 1]
:

(2.23)

\displaystyle(-\partial^{2}_{\tau})^{s}f(\tau)=c_{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f(\tau+h)-f(\tau)}{|h|^{1+2s}}dh,\quad\text{for $\tau\in\mathbb{R}$}.

We use the representation (
2.23
) in Section
4.2
.

\bullet
Poisson’s formula.
Consider the Fourier multiplier on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by
\frac{\sin(t|\nabla|)}{|\nabla|}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Then, from
[
34
, (27) on p. 74]
, it admits the following physical space representation as a convolution kernel:

(2.24)

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(t|\nabla|)}{|\nabla|}f=W(t,\cdot)*f

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and where the wave kernel
W
is defined as

(2.25)

\displaystyle W(t,x)=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{B(0,t)}(x)}{\sqrt{t^{2}-|x|^{2}}}

for any
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The identity (
2.24
) is often referred to as Poisson’s formula. Note that for a fixed function
f
, the function
g=\frac{\sin(t|\nabla|)}{|\nabla|}f
is the solution to Cauchy problem for the linear wave equation:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}g-\Delta g=0,\\
(g,\partial_{t}g)|_{t=0}=(0,f),\end{cases}\qquad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

\bullet
Hyperbolic Riesz potentials.

Next, we introduce the
hyperbolic Riesz potential

which plays a fundamental role in our analysis.
Let
\Box
be
the d’Alembertian given by

(2.26)

\displaystyle\Box=\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta.

Then,
given
b\in\mathbb{R}
,
we define
the hyperbolic Riesz potential
\Box^{b}

to be
the following space-time Fourier multiplier operator
with the following multiplier:

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\big[\Box^{b}u\big](\tau,\zeta)=\mathfrak{q}_{b}(\tau,\zeta)\big|\tau^{2}-|\zeta|^{2}\big|^{b}\widehat{u}(\tau,\zeta),\quad(\tau,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Here, the space-time multiplier
\mathfrak{q}_{b}(\tau,\zeta)
is given by

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{b}(\tau,\zeta)=\begin{cases}e^{-b\pi i\cdot\text{sgn}(\tau)},&\text{if }|\tau|\geq|\zeta|,\\
1,&\text{if }|\tau|<|\zeta|,\end{cases}\quad\text{where }\ \text{sgn}(\tau)=\begin{cases}1,&\tau\geq 0,\\
-1,&\tau<0.\end{cases}

See
[
85
, (28.28)]
with
\alpha=-2b
.
7
7

7

Note
that our sign conventions are slightly different from

[
85
, Subsection 28.1]
.
Moreover, there is a sign mistake in
[
85
, (28.28)]
.

For example, when
b=1
,
it follows from (
2.28
) that

\mathfrak{q}_{1}(\tau,\zeta)\big|\tau^{2}-|\zeta|^{2}\big|=-\tau^{2}+|\zeta|^{2}

for
\tau\neq 0
,
which corresponds almost everywhere to the symbol for the standard d’Alembertian
\Box
in (
2.26
).
Note that the hyperbolic Riesz potential satisfies
the semigroup property:

\Box^{b_{1}}\Box^{b_{2}}=\Box^{b_{1}+b_{2}}

for any
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.
From (
2.28
),
we see that the multiplier
\mathfrak{q}_{b}
and its inverse
\mathfrak{q}_{b}^{-1}

can be written as a linear combination of the form

(2.29)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}+\lambda_{2}\mathcal{C}+\lambda_{3}

for some inessential constants
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}\in\mathbb{C}
,
where
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{C}
are respectively the (temporal) Hilbert transform and the (sharp) cone multipliers defined by

(2.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\big(\mathcal{H}u\big)(\tau,\zeta)

\displaystyle=-i\text{sgn}(\tau)\widehat{u}(\tau,\zeta),

(2.31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\big(\mathcal{C}u\big)(\tau,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{|\tau|>|\zeta|}\widehat{u}(\tau,\zeta).

Remark 2.1

(unboundedness of the cone multiplier)
.

We note that the cone multiplier
\mathcal{C}
in (
2.31
)
is unbounded
in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
1<p\neq 2<\infty
.
Indeed, the unboundedness of
the cone multiplier
\mathcal{C}

follows from the
unboundedness
of the (sharp) ball multiplier
\mathcal{B}
,
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{x}\big(\mathcal{B}f\big)(\zeta)=\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(\zeta)\widehat{f}(\zeta),

in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
1<p\neq 2<\infty

due to Fefferman
[
35
]
;
see
[
68
,
62
]
.
See also
[
24
, Proposition 3.2 on p.374]
8
8

8

Strictly speaking,
Proposition 3.2
in
[
24
]
is not directly applicable
but one can proceed with a limiting argument.

for such an argument. We thus need to proceed with care when estimating objects involving the symbol
\mathfrak{q}_{b}
in (
2.28
).

For
b<-\frac{1}{2}
, the hyperbolic Riesz potential
\Box^{b}

admits the following physical side representation as a convolution operator
(see
[
85
, (28.21)]
):

(2.32)

\displaystyle(\Box^{b}u)(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathfrak{K}_{b}(t-t^{\prime},x-y)u(t^{\prime},y)dt^{\prime}dy,

where the kernel
\mathfrak{K}
is given by

(2.33)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{b}(t,x)=c_{b}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{t\geq 0}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,t)}(x)}{(|t|^{2}-|x|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}+b}},\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

See also
[
85
, (28.19)]
, where the condition
b<-\frac{1}{2}
appears.
Compare (
2.32
) (when
b=-1
) with Poisson’s formula
for a solution to the wave equation on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
; see (
2.24
)-(
2.25
) above.
See
[
85
, Subsection 28.1]

for a further discussion.

Now, consider the Fourier multiplier on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}
given by

(2.34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\big(\Box_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}^{b}u\big)(\tau,n)=\mathfrak{q}_{b}(\tau,n)\big|\tau^{2}-|n|^{2}\big|^{b}\widehat{u}(\tau,n),\quad(\tau,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

When there is no possible confusion, we also write
\Box^{b}
for
\Box^{b}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}
. From the Poisson formula (
2.9
) and an approximation argument (see for instance
[
78
, Lemma 2.5]
), the convolution kernel
\mathfrak{K}^{\mathbb{T}^{2}}_{b}
of
\Box_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}^{b}
is given by

(2.35)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}^{\mathbb{T}^{2}}_{b}(t,x)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathfrak{K}_{b}(t,x+2\pi m)

for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\cong\mathbb{R}\times[-\pi,\pi)^{2}
. Note that the sum in (
2.35
) is finite since the spatial support of
\mathfrak{K}_{b}
is included in
B(0,|t|)
. In particular, if
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R})
then we have

(2.36)

\displaystyle(\Box^{b}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}u)(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathfrak{K}_{b}(t-t^{\prime},x-y)u(t^{\prime},y)dt^{\prime}dy

for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times[-\pi,\pi)^{2}
and where the function
u(t^{\prime},\cdot)
is viewed as a
2\pi
-periodic function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

\bullet
Frequency projectors and paraproducts.

In the following, we define various frequency projectors and paraproducts.
Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])
be a smooth and symmetric bump function such that

\varphi(\tau)=\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{if $|\tau|\leq\frac{5}{4}$},\\
0\quad&\text{if $|\tau|>\frac{8}{5}$}.\end{cases}

Then, we define
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2};[0,1])
and
(\eta,\psi)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])^{2}

by setting

(2.37)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi(\zeta)&=\varphi(|\zeta|)-\varphi(2|\zeta|),\\
\eta(\tau)&=\varphi(\tau)-\varphi(2\tau),\\
\psi(\tau)&=\varphi(\tau)-\varphi(2\tau).\end{split}

Obviously, we have
\eta=\psi
.
We however, introduce these two functions
since we use
\eta
for localization
in temporal frequencies, while
we use
\psi
for localization in modulation (namely, the variable
|\tau|-|\zeta|
).

For any dyadic numbers
N,R,L\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
, we define the following
Littlewood-Paley frequency projectors:

(2.38)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\big(\mathbf{P}_{N}u\big)(\tau,n)

\displaystyle=\phi\Big(\frac{\zeta}{N}\Big)\widehat{u}(\tau,n),

(2.39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\big(\mathbf{T}_{R}u\big)(\tau,n)

\displaystyle=\eta\Big(\frac{\tau}{R}\Big)\widehat{u}(\tau,n),

(2.40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\big(\mathbf{M}_{N,R,L}u\big)(\tau,n)

\displaystyle=\phi\Big(\frac{\zeta}{N}\Big)\eta\Big(\frac{\tau}{R}\Big)\psi\Big(\frac{|\tau|-|\zeta|}{L}\Big)\widehat{u}(\tau,n)

for
(\tau,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. By construction, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbf{P}_{N}=\sum_{R\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbf{T}_{R}=\sum_{(N,R,L)\in(2^{\mathbb{Z}})^{3}}\mathbf{M}_{N,R,L}=\mathrm{Id}.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{N}
and
\mathcal{T}_{R}
be the respective convolution kernels of
\mathbf{P}_{N}
and
\mathbf{T}_{R}
defined above. By integration by parts, it is easy to see that for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

snd
A\geq 1
, we have

(2.41)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\mathcal{K}_{N}(x)|\lesssim_{\alpha,A}N^{|\alpha|+2}\langle Nx\rangle^{-A},\\
&|\partial^{k}_{t}\mathcal{T}_{R}(t)|\lesssim_{k,A}N^{k+1}\langle Rt\rangle^{-A}\end{split}

for all
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

We set

(2.42)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}\\
N\leq 1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}_{N}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}\\
N>1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}_{N}.

We also introduce the following space-time frequency projectors,
allowing us to compare spatial and temporal frequencies:

(2.43)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbf{Q}^{\text{hi,hi}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
|j-k|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\mathbf{T}_{2^{k}}\mathbf{P}_{2^{j}},\quad\mathbf{Q}^{\text{hi,lo}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
k>j+2\end{subarray}}\mathbf{T}_{2^{k}}\mathbf{P}_{2^{j}},\\
\text{and}\quad&\mathbf{Q}^{\text{lo,hi}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
k<j-2\end{subarray}}\mathbf{T}_{2^{k}}\mathbf{P}_{2^{j}},\end{split}

As a direct consequence of the Hörmander-Mihlin multiplier theorem, the operators
\mathbf{P}_{N}
,
\mathbf{T}_{R}
,
\mathbf{Q}^{\text{hi,hi}}
,
\mathbf{Q}^{\text{lo,hi}}
and
\mathbf{Q}^{\text{hi,lo}}
bounded in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for any
1<p<\infty
. Since the symbol
\psi(|\tau|-|\zeta|)
does not decay when
(\tau,\zeta)
is close to the light cone
\{(\tau,\zeta):|\tau|=|\zeta|\}
, the Hörmander-Mikhlin multiplier theorem is
not
applicable to
\mathbf{M}_{N,R,L}
.

Finally,
for
\gamma>0
, we define the following
\gamma
-dependent (spatial) paraproducts:

(2.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{>}_{\gamma}(u,v)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(N_{1},N_{2})\in(2^{\mathbb{Z}})^{2}\\
N_{1}\geq N_{2}^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}u\cdot\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}v,

(2.45)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{<}_{\gamma}(u,v)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(N_{1},N_{2})\in(2^{\mathbb{Z}})^{2}\\
N_{1}<N_{2}^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}u\cdot\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}v.

Note that
we have
uv=\mathcal{P}^{>}_{\gamma}(u,v)+\mathcal{P}^{<}_{\gamma}(u,v)
.
for any space-time functions
u
and
v
.

2.3.
Function spaces and linear estimates

In this subsection, we define the function spaces used in this work and study their properties.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
. We define the spaces
X^{s,b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
and
Y^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
as the completions of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
under the norms

(2.46)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}

\displaystyle:=\|\langle\zeta\rangle^{s}\langle|\tau|-|\zeta|\rangle^{b}\widehat{u}(\tau,\zeta)\|_{L^{2}_{\zeta}L^{2}_{\tau}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})},

(2.47)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{Y_{a}^{s,b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}

\displaystyle:=\big\|\langle t\rangle^{a}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{t,x}\big(\langle\zeta\rangle^{s}\,||\tau|-|\zeta||^{b}\widehat{u}(\tau,\zeta)\big)\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}.

where
\Box^{b}
is as in (
2.34
). We also use the shorthand notation
Y^{s,b}_{p}
and
Y^{s,b}_{a}
for
X^{s,b}
and
Y^{s,b}_{a}
, respectively.

Remark 2.3
.

Our choice of having a homogeneous modulation symbol
\big||\tau|-|\zeta|\big|
in the
Y^{s,b}_{a}
-norm
is motivated by the fact that
the hyperbolic Riesz potential

\Box^{b}
in (
2.27
) depends on the homogeneous symbol
\big||\tau|^{2}-|\zeta|^{2}\big|
; see (
2.28
) and (
2.34
). Working with a homogeneous modulation weight is also useful in our nonlinear analysis, see the proof of Lemma
4.6
.

In this work it is also convenient to work with the following anisotropic Sobolev spaces.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
(s,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
. We define the space
\Lambda^{s,b}_{p}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
as the completion of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
under the norm

(2.48)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\Lambda^{s,b}_{p}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}:=\big\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\langle\partial_{t}\rangle^{b}u\big\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}.

For an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, we define the restriction
X^{s,b}(I)

of the space
X^{s,b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})

onto interval

I
via the norm:

(2.49)

\|u\|_{X^{s,b}(I)}:=\inf\big\{\|v\|_{X^{s,b}}:v|_{I\times\mathbb{T}^{2}}=u\big\}.

When
I=[0,T]
for
T>0
, we use the shorthand notation
X^{s,b}_{T}
for
X^{s,b}([0,T])
.

Remark 2.5
.

Let
B(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})
be a space of space-time functions and
I\subset\mathbb{R}
an interval. In Section
5
, we use the notation
B(I)
to denote the subspace

(2.50)

\displaystyle\{u\in B(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}):\|\mathbf{1}_{I}(t)u\|_{B(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}^{2})}<\infty\}.

If
B=X^{s,b}
, then the two spaces (
2.49
) and (
2.50
) coincide for
b<\frac{1}{2}
; see Lemma
2.12
(ii).

We borrow the following gluing lemma from
[
13
, Lemma 4.5]
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\frac{1}{2}<b<1
and
I_{1},I_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}
be bounded intervals such that
I_{1}\cap I_{2}\neq\emptyset
. Then, we have

(2.51)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b}(I_{1}\cup I_{2})}\lesssim|I_{1}\cap I_{2}|^{\frac{1}{2}-b}\big(\|u\|_{X^{s,b}_{I_{1}}}+\|u\|_{X^{s,b}_{I_{2}}}\big).

Consider the nonhomogeneous linear damped wave equation:

(2.52)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u=F\\
(u,\partial_{t}u)|_{t=0}=(u_{0},v_{0}),\end{cases}\qquad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}.

The solution to (
2.52
) is given by

(2.53)

\displaystyle u(t)=\mathcal{U}(t)(u_{0},v_{0})+\mathcal{I}(F)(t),\quad t\in\mathbb{R}_{+},

where
\mathcal{U}(t)
is the linear operator

(2.54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(t)(u_{0},v_{0})=\partial_{t}\mathcal{D}(t)u_{0}+\mathcal{D}(t)(u_{0}+v_{0})

and
\mathcal{I}
is as in (
1.35
). We now state linear estimates in
X^{s,b}
-spaces for the problem (
2.52
). To this end, we need to extend the definitions of the linear operators
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{I}
to the whole real line in an appropriate way. See
[
69
,
63
,
100
]
for similar issues. Define the operators
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
by

(2.55)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t)(u_{0},v_{0})=\Big(e^{-\frac{|t|}{2}}\cos(t[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt])u_{0}+e^{-\frac{|t|}{2}}\frac{\sin(t[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt])}{2[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt]}\Big)u_{0}+e^{-\frac{|t|}{2}}\frac{\sin(t[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt])}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt]}v_{0},\quad t\in\mathbb{R},

and

(2.56)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(F)(t)=\frac{1}{2i}\big(\mathcal{I}_{+}(F)(t)-\mathcal{I}_{-}(F)(t)\big),\quad t\in\mathbb{R},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\pm}(F)(t)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{e_{n}(x)}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{it\mu}-e^{-\frac{|t|}{2}\pm it[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]}}{\frac{1}{2}+i\mu\mp i[\hskip-1.70717pt[n]\hskip-1.70717pt]}\widehat{F}(\mu,n)d\mu.

One then observes that
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t)(u_{0},v_{0})=\mathcal{U}(t)(u_{0},v_{0})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(F)(t)=\mathcal{I}(F)(t)
for any
t\geq 0
(see
[
63
, page 16]
).

We first state the linear homogeneous estimate for
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t)
. See
[
63
, Lemma 2.7]
for a proof.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
b<\frac{3}{2}
and
I
be an interval. Then, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t)(u_{0},v_{0})\big\|_{X^{s,b}(I)}\lesssim(1+|I|)\|(u_{0},v_{0})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}.

Next, we recall the linear nonhomogeneous estimate for the modified Duhamel operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
. See
[
63
, Lemma 2.8]
for a proof in the identical three-dimensional case.

Lemma 2.8
.

Fix
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\frac{1}{2}<b<1
and an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
0\leq|I|\leq 1
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}(F)\big\|_{X^{s,b}(I)}\lesssim\|F\|_{X^{s-1,b-1}(I)}.

Lastly, we record the following time localization estimate. See
[
63
, Lemma 2.9]
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
-\frac{1}{2}<b_{1}<b_{2}<\frac{1}{2}
and
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a closed interval. Then, we have

(2.57)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b_{1}}(I)}\lesssim|I|^{b_{2}-b_{1}}\|u\|_{X^{s,b_{2}}(I)}.

We now recall the Strichartz estimates
for the linear wave equation.
Given
0<s<1
,
we say that a pair
(q,r)
is
s
-admissible
(a pair
(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{r})
is dual
s
-admissible,
9
9

9

Here, we define
the notion of dual
s
-admissibility for the convenience of the presentation.
Note that
(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{r})
is dual
s
-admissible
if and only if
(\widetilde{q}^{\prime},\widetilde{r}^{\prime})
is
(1-s)
-admissible.

respectively)
if
1\leq\widetilde{q}<2<q\leq\infty
,

1<\widetilde{r}\leq 2\leq r<\infty
,

(2.58)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}+\frac{2}{r}=1-s=\frac{1}{\widetilde{q}}+\frac{2}{\widetilde{r}}-2,\qquad\frac{2}{q}+\frac{1}{r}\leq\frac{1}{2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{2}{\widetilde{q}}+\frac{1}{\widetilde{r}}\geq\frac{5}{2}.

We refer to the first two equalities as the scaling conditions
and the last two inequalities as the admissibility conditions.

Let us now state a lemma, providing a more direct description of the admissible exponents;
see
[
44
, Lemma 3.1]

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
0<s<1
.
A pair
(q,r)
is
s
-admissible if

(2.59)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}+\frac{2}{r}=1-s\qquad\text{and}\qquad 2\leq r\leq\begin{cases}\frac{6}{3-4s},&\text{if }s<\frac{3}{4},\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

A pair
(\widetilde{q},\widetilde{r})
is dual
s
-admissible if

(2.60)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{q}}+\frac{2}{\widetilde{r}}=3-s\qquad\text{and}\qquad\max\bigg\{1+,\frac{6}{7-4s}\bigg\}\leq\widetilde{r}\leq\frac{2}{2-s}.

The Strichartz estimates on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
have been studied by many
mathematicians. See Ginibre-Velo
[
40
]
, Lindblad-Sogge
[
64
]
,
and Keel-Tao
[
55
]
.
and the finite speed of propagation for the wave equation.

The transference principle (
[
58
, Theorem 3.2]
).
See also
[
92
, Lemma 2.9]
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Given
0<s<1
, let
(q,r)
be
s
-admissible. Fix
0<T\leq 1
. Then, for
b>\frac{1}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{q}_{t}([0,T];L^{r}_{x}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))}\lesssim\|u\|_{X^{s,b}_{T}}.

In particular by (
2.59
) and Lemma
2.11
, we have the following estimate for
0<\delta<\frac{1}{4}
:

(2.61)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L_{t}^{\frac{6}{1+2\delta}}([0,T];L^{\frac{6}{1-4\delta}}_{x}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))}\lesssim\|u\|_{X^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta,\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}_{T}},

for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Lastly, we state a result on the boundedness of the multiplication with smooth functions of the time variable on the spaces defined above.

Lemma 2.12
.

For any
\lambda\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, the following bounds hold:

(i)
For any
b\in\mathbb{R}
and
1<p<\infty
, we have

(2.62)

\displaystyle\|\lambda(t)u\|_{\Lambda^{s,b}_{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u\|_{\Lambda^{s,b}_{p}},

(2.63)

\displaystyle\|\lambda(t)u\|_{X^{s,b}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u\|_{X^{s,b}}.

(ii)
For any
-\frac{1}{2}<b<\frac{1}{2}
and interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, we have

(2.64)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b}(I)}\sim\|\mathbf{1}_{I}(t)u\|_{X^{s,b}}.

Proof.

See
[
92
, Lemma 2.11]
and
[
13
, Lemma 4.4]
for proofs of (
2.63
) and (
2.64
). The bound (
2.62
) follows from standard product estimates.
∎

3.
Elliptic and hyperbolic singular kernels

In this section, we prove technical lemmas on various convolutions with singular kernels which exhibit singularities at either a point or on a light cone. These results are crucial in Section
5
to study regularity properties of the stochastic convolution and the imaginary Gaussian multiplicative chaos.

Let us note that in this section, some results are either stated for the periodic or the full space settings (or for both).

3.1.
Elliptic singularities

We first study singularities which are of elliptic type; namely, functions which are singular at a single point. Many of the results which follow deal with estimates on convolutions of such singularities with bump functions
\nu_{N}
which satisfy a decay condition, see (
3.1
) and (
3.7
) below. We note that in the case when
\nu_{N}
is actually compactly supported, some of these results are already essentially proved in the literature on parabolic singular stochastic partial differential equations; see for instance
[
50
]
.

We first prove an estimate on smoothed elliptic singularities.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\theta\in(0,2)
. Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\nu_{N}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function satisfying the bound

(3.1)

\displaystyle|\nu_{N}(x)|\lesssim_{A}N^{2}\langle Nx\rangle^{-A}

for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
and any finite
A\geq 1
. Then, we have

(3.2)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\nu_{N}(x-y)|y|^{-\theta}dy\lesssim\min(N,|x|^{-1})^{\theta},

for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
, with an implicit constant independent of
N
.

Proof.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
. We separately estimate
the contributions from
|x-y|\lesssim N^{-1}

and
|x-y|\gg N^{-1}
to the integral

\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x):=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\nu_{N}(x-y)|y|^{-\theta}dy.

\bullet

Case 1:
|x-y|\lesssim N^{-1}
.
We first consider the case
|x|\lesssim N^{-1}
.
Then by (
3.1
), the contribution to
\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)
in this case is bounded by

(3.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)\lesssim N^{2}\int_{B(0,cN^{-1})}|y|^{-\theta}dy\lesssim N^{\theta}=\min(N,|x|^{-1})^{\theta}.

Next, suppose that
|x|\gg N^{-1}
.
Since
|x-y|\lesssim N^{-1}
,
we have
|x|\sim|y|\gg N^{-1}
.
Thus, from (
3.1
), the contribution to
\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)
in this case is bounded by

(3.4)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)\lesssim N^{2}|x|^{-\theta}\int_{B(x,10N^{-1})}dy\lesssim|x|^{-\theta}=\min(N,|x|^{-1})^{\theta}.

\bullet

Case 2:
|x-y|\gg N^{-1}
.
In this case, we have

\langle N(x-y)\rangle\sim N|x-y|
.
We first consider the case
|x|\ll N^{-1}
.
By (
3.1
) with
|y|\sim|x-y|\gg N^{-1}
,
the contribution to
\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)
in this case is bounded by

(3.5)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)\lesssim N^{-8}\int_{|y|\gg N^{-1}}|y|^{-10-\theta}dy\lesssim N^{\theta}=\min(N,|x|^{-1})^{\theta}.

Next, we consider the case
|x|\gtrsim N^{-1}
.
By (
3.1
) and estimating separately the cases
(i)
|x|\lesssim|y|

and (ii)
|x|\gg|y|
(which implies
|x|\sim|x-y|
),
we bound the contribution to
\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)
in this case by

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)&\lesssim N^{-8}|x|^{-\theta}\intt_{|x-y|\gg N^{-1}}|x-y|^{-10}dy+N^{-8}|x|^{-10}\intt_{|y|\ll|x|}|y|^{-\theta}dy\\
&\lesssim|x|^{-\theta}=\min(N,|x|^{-1})^{\theta}.\end{split}

By putting (
3.3
),
(
3.4
), (
3.5
), and (
3.6
) together,
we obtain (
3.2
).
∎

Note that in view of (
2.15
), the Green’s function (
2.13
) has an elliptic singularity at the origin. In the next lemma, we prove various bounds on smoothed Green’s functions.

Lemma 3.2
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\nu_{N}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
be a function satisfying the bound

(3.7)

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\nu_{N}(x)|\lesssim_{A}N^{|\alpha|+2}\langle Nx\rangle^{-A}

for all
A\geq 1
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
. Let
G
be the Green’s function (
2.13
). Then, the following bounds hold.

(i)
Set
G_{N}=G*\nu_{N}
. Then, we have

(3.8)

\displaystyle|G_{N}(x)|\lesssim\begin{cases}\bigl\langle\,\log\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)\bigr\rangle\quad&\text{for $|x|<2$},\\
\langle x\rangle^{-A}\quad&\text{for $|x|\geq 2$}\\
\end{cases}

for any
A\geq 1
and

(3.9)

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}G_{N}(x)|\lesssim\begin{cases}\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)^{-|\alpha|}\big(1+\bigl\langle\,\log\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)\bigr\rangle\mathbf{1}_{|\alpha|=2}\big)\quad&\text{for $|x|<2$},\\
\langle x\rangle^{-A}\quad&\text{for $|x|\geq 2$}\\
\end{cases}

for any
A\geq 1
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
with
1\leq|\alpha|\leq 2
. Here, the implicit constants are independent of
N
.

(ii)
Set
\widetilde{G}_{N}=G*G*\nu_{N}
. Then, we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle\big|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\widetilde{G}_{N}(x)\big|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-A}

for any
A\geq 1
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
with
0\leq|\alpha|\leq 1
and

(3.11)

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\widetilde{G}_{N}(x)\big|\lesssim\begin{cases}\bigl\langle\,\log\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)\bigr\rangle\quad&\text{for $|x|<2$},\\
\langle x\rangle^{-A}\quad&\text{for $|x|\geq 2$}\\
\end{cases}

for any
A\geq 1
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
with
|\alpha|=2
. Here, the implicit constants are independent of
N
.

Proof.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. We first prove (i). Let
\lambda\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
be a smooth bump function such that

\displaystyle\lambda(x)=\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{if $|x|<1$},\\
0\quad&\text{if $|x|\geq 2$}.\end{cases}

We decompose
G_{N}
as follows:

(3.12)

\displaystyle G_{N}=(\lambda G)*\nu_{N}+\big((1-\lambda)G\big)*\nu_{N}=:\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}.

Let us focus on (
3.8
). By (
3.12
), it suffices to prove (
3.8
) with
G_{N}
replaced with
I
and
I I
.

Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By (
2.15
), we have that

(3.13)

\displaystyle|\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)|\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigl\langle\,\log(|x-y|)\bigr\rangle\,\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|\leq 2}\,|\nu_{N}(y)|dy.

If
|x|\lesssim 1
, then (
3.13
) and arguments similar to those in the proof of Lemma
3.1
show

(3.14)

\displaystyle|\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)|\lesssim\int_{B(0,10)}\bigl\langle\,\log\big(|x-y|\big)\bigr\rangle|\nu_{N}(y)|dy\lesssim\bigl\langle\,\log\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)\bigr\rangle.

If
|x|\gg 1
, then we note that
|x|\sim|y|\gtrsim 1
for each
y
in the support of the integrand of
\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)
. Hence, by (
3.13
) and (
3.7
), we have

(3.15)

\displaystyle|\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt(x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{2-A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigl\langle\,\log\big(|x-y|\big)\bigr\rangle\,\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|\leq 2}\,\langle y\rangle^{-A}dy\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-A}

for each
A\geq 2
. Thus, (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) show (
3.8
) for
I
. By (
2.15
) and similar arguments, we also have that

\displaystyle|\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-A}

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
A\geq 2
. This shows (
3.8
) for
I I
and finishes the proof of (
3.8
).

Next, we prove (
3.9
). Fix a multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
. If
|\alpha|=1
, then (
3.9
) follows from the equality

\partial^{\alpha}_{x}G_{N} =(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}G)*\nu_{N},

the bound (
2.15
) and by arguing as in the proof of (
3.8
).

Now we prove (
3.9
) for
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
and
|\alpha|=2
. In this case, we have to proceed with care since the distribution
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}G
is not locally integrable near the origin; see (
2.15
). Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
such that
\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
and
|\alpha_{1}|=|\alpha_{2}|=1
. In particular, we have

(3.16)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt=\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\big\{\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{x}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt\big\}=\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\big\{\big(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\lambda G)\big)*\nu_{N}\big\}.

Next, we decompose
\big(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\lambda G)\big)*\nu_{N}
as follows:

(3.17)

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\lambda G)\big)*\nu_{N}=\big(\lambda(N\cdot)\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\lambda G)\big)*\nu_{N}+\big((1-\lambda)(N\cdot)\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\lambda G)\big)*\nu_{N}=:\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}+\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}.

Therefore, by (
3.12
), (
3.16
) and (
3.17
), (
3.9
) (with
|\alpha|=2
) follows from the bound

(3.18)

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt^{\alpha_{1}}_{1}(x)|+|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt^{\alpha_{1}}_{2}(x)|+|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}(x)|\lesssim\begin{cases}\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)^{-2}\bigl\langle\,\log\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)\bigr\rangle\quad&\text{for $|x|<2$},\\
\langle x\rangle^{-A}\quad&\text{for $|x|\geq 2$}\\
\end{cases}

for any
A\geq 1
.

We first consider the contribution of
\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}
. Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By (
2.15
), we have that

(3.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}(x)|&=\big|\big(\big(\lambda(N\cdot)\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\lambda G)\big)*\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\nu_{N}\big)(x)\big|\\
&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x-y|^{-1}\,\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|\lesssim N^{-1}}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\nu_{N}(y)|dy.\end{split}

If
|x|\lesssim N^{-1}
, then we have
|y|\lesssim N^{-1}
for each
y
in the support of the integrand of
\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}(x)
. Hence, by (
3.7
) and (
3.19
), we have

(3.20)

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}(x)|\lesssim N^{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x-y|^{-1}\,\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|\lesssim N^{-1}}dy\lesssim N^{2}\sim\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)^{2}.

Otherwise,
|x|\gg N^{-1}
and
|y|\sim|x|
for each
y
in the support of the integrand of
\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}(x)
. In this case, (
3.7
) and (
3.19
) imply that

(3.21)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}(x)|&\lesssim N^{3-A}|x|^{-A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x-y|^{-1}\,\mathbf{1}_{|x-y|\lesssim N^{-1}}dy\\
&\lesssim N^{2-A}|x|^{-A}\\
&\lesssim\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)^{-2}\mathbf{1}_{N^{-1}\lesssim|x|\lesssim 1}+\langle x\rangle^{-A}\mathbf{1}_{|x|\gg 1}\end{split}

for any
A\geq 2
. Combining (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) yields the bound

(3.22)

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt^{\alpha_{1}}_{1}(x)|\lesssim\begin{cases}\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)^{-2}\quad&\text{for $|x|<2$},\\
\langle x\rangle^{-A}\quad&\text{for $|x|\geq 2$}\\
\end{cases}

for any
A\geq 1
.

We now consider the contribution of
\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}
. Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By (
2.15
), we have that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}(x)|&=\big|\big(\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\big\{(1-\lambda)(N\cdot)\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}(\lambda G)\big\}\big)*\nu_{N}\big)(x)\big|\\
&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x-y|^{-2}\,\mathbf{1}_{N^{-1}\lesssim|x-y|\lesssim 1}|\nu_{N}(y)|dy.\end{split}

In the above, we used the chain rule and the fact that the support of
\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}(1-\lambda)
is included in the set
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x|\sim 1\}
so that

\big|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\big\{(1-\lambda)(N\cdot)\big\}(z)\big| \sim N\big|\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\lambda(Nz)\big|\sim|z|^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{|z|\sim N^{-1}}

for any
z
in the support of
\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}(1-\lambda)
. If
|x|\lesssim N^{-1}
, then by (
3.7
) and (
3.23
), we have

(3.24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}(x)|&\lesssim N^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x-y|^{-2}\,\mathbf{1}_{N^{-1}\lesssim|x-y|\lesssim 1}dy\\
&\lesssim N^{2}\langle\,\log(N)\rangle\\
&\lesssim\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)^{-2}\bigl\langle\,\log\big(|x|+N^{-1}\big)\bigr\rangle.\end{split}

Otherwise, we have
|x|\gg N^{-1}
. Let
y
be in the support of the integrand of
\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{2}(x)
. Assume that
|y|\gtrsim|x|
. Let
A\geq 2
. From (
3.23
) and (
3.7
), we then have

(3.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{2}^{\alpha_{1}}(x)|&\lesssim N^{2-A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x-y|^{-2}\,\mathbf{1}_{N^{-1}\lesssim|x-y|\lesssim 1}\,\mathbf{1}_{|y|\gtrsim|x|}\,|y|^{-A}dy\\
&\lesssim N^{2-A}|x|^{-A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x-y|^{-2}\,\mathbf{1}_{N^{-1}\lesssim|x-y|\lesssim 1}\,\mathbf{1}_{|y|\gtrsim|x|}dy.\end{split}

If
|y|\sim|x|
for
y
in the support of the integrand of
\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.56905pt\text{I}\hskip 0.56905pt_{2}(x)
, then we throw away the factor
|x-y|^{-2}
in (
3.25
) via the bound
|x-y|\gtrsim N^{-1}
and get

(3.26)
